 Mellow In My Mind (6:12)
 [link: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1177487-01-01-02.mp3] [audio: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1177487-01-01-02.mp3] 
 Genetic Force (4:56)
 [link: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1177487-01-02-01.mp3] [audio: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1177487-01-02-01.mp3] 
 Ruined Tower (6:55)
 [link: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1177487-01-02-02.mp3] [audio: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1177487-01-02-02.mp3] 
 Cloud Sea (4:41)
 Review: Mukatsuku's endless thirst for archival gold and knack for killer reissues burns strong with this latest hand-numbered 12", which digs deep into classic ambient techno from the Far East. The man behind the music is Virgo, a solo project by Yasutaka Sato that was active from 1996 to 2004 and then reappeared in 2024. This EP revisit some of his best moments from more than two decades ago, starting with the soothing, pulse-slowing beauty of 'Mellow In My Mind' before cosmic charm defines 'Genetic Force' and 'Ruined Tower' has a more melancholic, sombre feel. 'Cloud Sea' lifts the mood with a gently hopeful closer with soft new age chords and oh-so gentle rhythms that break like waves on a distant island paradise. … Read more

 NK012B02 (9:05)
 Review: Three Berlin-based improvisers sat down for the first time with one stereo microphone in the middle of a room and just played. Aidan Baker of Nadja, percussionist Andrea Belfi and Erik Skodvin of Svarte Greiner and Deaf Center recorded NK012 that way, and its five pieces slowly form through guitars, pedals, percussion and synths, with looped phrases piling up into thicker drones. Umor Rex first put it out on cassette in 2015, and Rafael Anton Irisarri has now remastered it for its first vinyl pressing. It's the third of four 20th-anniversary reissues from the label. … Read more

 Lily (3:02)
 Review: Mark Gomes is the Australian musician behind Blue Chemise and makes music at the edge of earshot. After his 2017 debut Influence on Dusk, pressed in limited quantities on his own label, he moved to Students of Decay with Daughters of Time - a set of short vignettes recorded straight to dictaphone. Mothers of All follows by trading those alien nursery songs for what the label calls spectral salon music, heard from far away in time and place. Gomes finds odd harmonies in how the various fragments sit together, and though the album is brief and private, it has feelings everyone relates to. … Read more

 Night Fog
 Review: Los Angeles based composer and electronic experimentalist Jeremiah Chiu has long been touted as one of the more exciting artists in contemporary music, in part for the ways in which he can fuse cutting edge analogue and modular synthesis and nods to a variety of electronic music styles with elements of jazz and neo-classical. Edge Conditions, his latest album, sees him lightly re-pivot following a string of electro-acoustic albums made in collaboration with the likes of violist Marta Sofia Honer and the late Ariel Kalma. Primarily electronic and avant-garde, but with plenty of memorable melodic motifs and flashes of acoustic instrumentation, it feels like a grown-up and expressly avant-garde affair that blurs the boundaries between Boards of Canada, Aphex Twin, Yasuaki Shimizu and Arthur Russell's more extended, freewheeling moments. … Read more

 Night Fog
 Review: The last time we dived into the world of Jeremiah Chiu it was on Electric Time, a record that came into being after he "walked into the Vintage Synthesizer Museum in Highland Park, Los Angeles, with no plan more specific than 'let's fire this stuff up and see what happens'." He came out the other end with a stunning ambient long player that felt, for want of a less cliched terms, organic. Now we're back with Edge Conditions, and while we don't know the back story this time we do know there are differences and similarities with the previous encounter. Again, Chiu exhibits a knack for letting musicality breathe properly. Nothing here feels rushed or overly filled. Everything in its right place across 11 tracks of crunchy downbeat, loose jazzy chill, and borderline abrasive percussive epics. With the odd slice of seriously immersive ambient, in the true sense, of course.
 … Read more

 A Floral Pattern
 [link: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1176487-01-02-05.mp3] [audio: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1176487-01-02-05.mp3] 
 Night Fog
 Review: Chicago gave Jeremiah Chiu the reference points and Los Angeles seems to have taught him how much air to leave between them; Edge Conditions is full of that push and pull. 'A.M. Fuses' starts with a bruised synth motif as processed drums ricochet across the stereo field, then settles into a lopsided techno-jazz lope that feels related to Tortoise without doing tribute-act homework. 'Plastic Mind/Elastic Machines' is better again: Booker Stardrum's low-end lands like soft machinery, modular chatter and train-whistle puffs fizzing above it before the whole track thins into a long, lucid melody. Chiu makes intricacy feel oddly casual. … Read more

 Where You Are Now, Voyager
 Review: This posthumously completed album finds Johann Johannsson and Adam Wiltzie recording together in 2013, trading ideas between Berlin and Belgium and sketching it out before Johannsson's death five years later. Adam Wiltzie returned to the tapes afterward, finishing the record between 2023 and 2025 with contributions from Tim Hecker and composer Bryan Senti - plus guitar parts recorded by Steve Albini shortly before his own death. Orchestral tape loop drone, built on ten tracks whose titles make for a private code between the two friends: see 'September Nineteen Sixty Nine', marking the month both men were born, two days and an ocean apart. … Read more

 From Dusk (5:30)
 Review: Grainy Film was already confirmed when Kotaro Maruyama's previous album, Vapor Curve, was stuck in manufacturing hell and suffering from a one-year delay to its release date. So you'd think the Tokyo artist has had plenty of time to go back and finesse any parts of this that perhaps weren't quite right, safe in the knowledge the predecessor would need at least some space and time to breathe. That probably wasn't the case, though, given this record was made entirely on hardware synths, samplers and delay units, and evolved organically during its sessions. Once the tracks are, they are, so to speak. And they very much are. The producer and musician has described what's here as "music for a cigar", and it's easy to grasp that concept. These aren't noises that insist on themselves, but textured soundscapes that set mood and create a rich atmosphere you want to bathe in.
 … Read more

 Divers Dub La Ver
 Review: Tokyo and Glasgow meet in a dub studio here as Taka Noda, who records as Mystica Tribe, drops a doozy. He launched the project in 2010 and had a track on Pangaea's FABRICLIVE 73 mix before releasing his debut album on Silent Season. He teams up with Scott Prentice, the Scottish artist behind Scopeotaku who works across industrial ambient to mutant dub and they combine heavyweight dub reggae with psychedelic electronics across eight tracks. They're all melodic, strange and slow-moving, from the pillowy 'Chrysalis' and 'Astronavigation' to the pensive 'Human Conditions' and the closing 'Divers Dub Long.' A real late-night charmer and dancefloor bender. … Read more

 We Should Keep Going
 Review: Purelink's music has always been about that liminal blur between presence and dissolving into the moment, and Signs pushes further into that territory than anything they've made before. The formerly Chicago, now New York-based, trio Tommy Paslaski, Ben Paulson and Akeem Asani work as a collective unit and you can feel their shared hand as nothing sounds like any one member's solo instinct, more like a third mind emerging between them. The tracks drift through glitch textures, dub-adjacent low end, and spacious ambient passages without settling anywhere too long. It's subdued one moment and strangely elevated the next and is music built from real-time chemistry rather than isolated production. … Read more

 Circle Of Dust
 Review: Faith catches Purelink during a moment of flux and rather than smoothing that over, they let it shape this 2025 LP's whole texture. Moving from Chicago to New York clearly cracked something open for this trio and where last album Signs felt built from three laptops in a living room, this one breathes with live instrumentation and, for the first time, real vocal presence. Loraine James floats through 'Rookie' like smoke over those familiar patient rhythms, while Angelina Nonaj's deadpan narration on 'First Iota' anchors the trio's more dissociated tendencies into something startlingly direct. 'Circle of Dust' closes things beautifully, kinetic and warm in equal measure. Their most emotionally exposed work yet. … Read more

 Uplands Unplugged
 Review: Enno Velthuys made music like a language of his own. The Hague-born musician, who died in 2009, lived for years in a small room in his mother's apartment and that room became the studio for this album. It was recorded entirely on a four-track Tascam cassette machine, and Willem de Ridder first played one of his tapes on the radio in 1980 before he went on to release a run of cassettes in the early 80s. Here, 50s instrumental guitar, Indorock, 60s beat, early ambient and western-film themes melt together with fury-warm nostalgia and a little sense of unease. Hessel Veldman named the tracks, since Enno never bothered. It's a lonely and tender listen. … Read more

 What Remains
 Review: French-born but Dusseldorf-based Mathias Villard has been part of the Affin label for a while. He is a drummer at heart but has submerged himself ever deeper in the electronic world and is now adept at introspective ambient and drone sounds that are all about escapism and immersion. Past Radiance is his debut album and across seven pieces he melts melancholia into deep low ends with decaying harmonies and widescreen spatial fields. Hints of melody flicker at the edges like half-remembered dreams, and filtered noise takes subtle cues from the worlds of ambient, drone and post-rock. It's a still, sparse world but one full of very real feelings. … Read more

 Der Letzte Raum (3:10)
 Review: Germany-born Vril has long made techno that feels like transmissions from another world, but KRUX finds the now Portugal-based producer opening the doors to new voices. The 13-track album retains his signature dub-heavy atmosphere while pushing into stranger, more unpredictable territory. Rene Wise joins for the churning pressure of 'Wave Collapse' while Aurora Halal and Verraco bring their own distinct energies to the fold. Across the record, machinery and human touch sit in uneasy balance, from shadowy hypnosis to more fractured rhythms. It's a compelling shift in direction. … Read more
